A vertical one-sheet poster uses a forensic, evidence-led creative device. A Chinese woman in her late twenties, an art conservator with a sharp, intelligent face and black hair tied in a neat low bun, wears a tailored linen smock. Her gaze is intensely focused, not on the viewer, but on the work before her. A Chinese man in his late forties, a scholarly mentor with graying hair at his temples and wearing a simple button-down shirt, stands behind her left shoulder, his expression one of sober concern. The central image is a contact-sheet-like grid of nine identical, tightly cropped photographic details of the same section of a painted silk scroll, arranged in three rows. Each detail shows the same patch of intricate, faded erotic imagery-entwined limbs, the curve of a bare back, the delicate fraying of silk threads. The grid floats against a deep, matte charcoal gray field. The woman's hands, covered in thin cotton conservation gloves, hold a precision scalpel and a soft brush, her tools poised at the edge of the central detail square, as if caught in the act of revealing more. Light falls from a single cool, clinical source at the top left, casting sharp shadows from the implied edges of the photographic 'frames' and glinting off her scalpel. The palette is restrained: the warm, aged gold and cinnabar red of the scroll details, the cool gray of the background, the black of her hair and his shirt, and the pale skin of her hands and his face. The title, THE SECOND LAYER, is set in a crisp, sans-serif typeface of monumental scale, occupying the upper third of the poster. Its letters are formed not by solid ink, but by a precise void-the deep gray background showing through where the title has been cleanly cut away from a top layer of smooth, off-white paper, the torn edges of the paper creating a subtle tactile border around each letter. The tagline is set in a smaller, classic serif font, aligned left in the negative space below the title's baseline: A discovery that demands a different kind of preservation. At the very bottom, in a compact, legible sans-serif block, is the release line COMING SOON and a fictional billing block. The entire poster has the controlled, anxious stillness of a laboratory, where a single decision hangs in the balance. The woman's partially unbuttoned smock reveals the collar of a silk blouse beneath, and the tension between her professional precision and the intimate, vulnerable imagery she uncovers is palpable. The man's proximity is professional, not intimate, his hand resting on the back of her chair, their bodies close but not touching, the conflict between duty and desire etched in the space between them. The country is China, the city is Shanghai, and the moment is contemporary. The conservator is Chinese, female, and in her late twenties. The scholar is Chinese, male, and in his late forties.: THE SECOND LAYER: top center, large-scale die-cut negative-space sans-serif type; A discovery that demands a different kind of preservation.: below title baseline left, classic serif; COMING SOON: bottom left, compact sans-serif; A LENG STUDIOS PRODUCTION: bottom center, compact sans-serif; JIA LI as THE CONSERVATOR: bottom center, compact sans-serif; WEN SHAO as THE SCHOLAR: bottom center, compact sans-serif; DIRECTED BY ANNA FENG: bottom center, compact sans-serif;
A vertical one-sheet poster employs a surreal transformation and tactile print object as its central creative device. A Chinese woman in her late twenties, the art conservator, is depicted from the waist up. Her black hair is down, slightly tousled, and she wears a contemporary silk camisole, the straps loose off her shoulders. Her eyes are closed, her expression one of absorbed surrender. A Chinese man in his late forties, the scholar, is seen only as a pair of hands entering the frame from behind her, his fingers gently tracing a path of deep, crimson-red ink-wash pigment directly onto the skin of her bare back. The painted stroke follows her spine, mimicking the fluid, organic line of a unfurling silk scroll. The background is a vast field of textured, handmade paper in a warm, creamy off-white. From the woman's shoulders and hair, delicate, intricate patterns in gold leaf and faded sepia seem to bleed outward, transforming into the illustrated landscape of a classical Chinese erotic scroll-suggestive mountain contours, flowing water motifs, and fragmented poetic couplets in elegant English script that merge with the pattern of her hair. This 'second layer' of imagery is rendered in a translucent, lacquered glaze over the photograph, creating a palpable tension between the realistic human form and the emerging painted fantasy. The light is soft, diffuse, and golden, as if from a late afternoon sun filtered through rice paper, illuminating the gloss of the ink on her skin and the metallic sheen of the gold leaf. The palette is sensual and warm: the cream of the paper, the warm bronze of her skin, the stark crimson of the ink, the antique gold of the leaf, and the dark sepia of the scroll details. The title, THE SECOND LAYER, is set in a serif typeface with calligraphic flourishes, appearing as if embossed with deep, physical pressure into the paper at the top of the poster, its letters casting soft, grazing shadows. The tagline is handwritten in a confident, cursive script that mirrors the ink-stroke on her back, flowing diagonally across the lower left quadrant: What is revealed when the surface is touched. At the bottom, in tiny, letterpress-style typography, is the release line FOR YOUR CONSIDERATION and a fictional, minimalist billing block. The poster is an object of sensual contemplation. The man's touch is deliberate and intimate, the application of pigment a conscious, collaborative act. The woman's arched back and parted lips signal a conscious, receptive participation. Their interaction is a silent dialogue about revelation and creation, with the historical scroll's passion literally being mapped onto her present body. The country is China, the setting is an abstracted studio space, and the time is contemporary. The conservator is Chinese, female, and in her late twenties. The scholar is Chinese, male, and in his late forties.: THE SECOND LAYER: top center, deeply embossed serif type with calligraphic details; What is revealed when the surface is touched.: lower left diagonal, cursive handwritten script; FOR YOUR CONSIDERATION: bottom center, tiny letterpress sans-serif; A LENG STUDIOS: bottom center, tiny letterpress sans-serif; JIA LI: bottom center, tiny letterpress sans-serif; WEN SHAO: bottom center, tiny letterpress sans-serif; ANNA FENG: bottom center, tiny letterpress sans-serif;
This theatrical poster for the fictional film 'The Second Layer' is a photographic split-world composition. It depicts the two Chinese adult protagonists side-by-side within their shared, contemporary Shanghai conservation studio at night. The setting is an artist's workroom with a long, industrial table bearing art restoration tools. The left half of the poster presents a controlled, sterile environment. On the left, the Chinese male conservator, late forties, stands. His mature face is clean-shaven, his black hair neatly combed. He wears a tailored, crisp white lab coat over a dark blue dress shirt, the top button fastened. His posture is erect and analytical, hands held at his sides. His expression is one of academic focus and severe restraint, his eyes directly confronting the viewer. He is bathed in the cool, clinical white light of an adjustable LED lamp on the table, which casts sharp, clean-edged shadows. To his right, the Chinese female conservator, in her late twenties, occupies the poster's right half. Her dark hair is tied back in a practical, slightly messy bun. She wears a silk blouse of deep plum, its top two buttons undone and collar askew, revealing a subtle curve of her collarbone and upper chest. The blouse is partially untucked from her tailored black trousers. Her posture is a tense, forward curve. Her right arm is extended, her hand pressing flat against a large, illuminated light table on which the Qing-era scroll lies partially unfurled. Her gaze is fixed not on the viewer but on the luminous surface, her expression a mixture of awe and apprehension. The scroll itself glows with warm, rich amber light from within the table, revealing detailed, painted scenes of intertwined figures in intimate, flowing embrace. The poster's split is defined by this dual lighting system: cold white on the left, warm amber bleeding into the right. The man's world is ordered, with rulers, magnifying glasses, and archival boxes visible; the woman's side is marked by scattered pigment powders, a discarded artist's glove, and the ghostly, overlapping digital scan lines of a monitor displaying the scroll's hidden layer. The background wall is a rough, exposed brick painted white, its texture prominent. The color palette is a deliberate duotone conflict of stark, near-blueshifted white against deep, sensual amber and plum. The title typography acts as a physical threshold. The words THE SECOND LAYER are rendered in monumental, crisp sans-serif capitals, printed in a deep, semi-gloss black. They are positioned horizontally across the center of the poster, directly over the boundary line between the two halves. The letters physically interact with the scene: the 'O' in 'SECOND' is perfectly aligned over the glowing light table, its counter forming a window that frames the woman's hand pressing on the scroll. The 'R' in 'LAYER' extends down behind the man's shoulder, integrating with the architecture. Below, a secondary tagline in a lighter, italic serif reads: WHAT DO YOU PRESERVE? in the same deep black, aligned left over the woman's half. The fictional release line COMING SOON sits small and unobtrusive in the bottom left corner. The billing block, a compact cluster of tiny, justified uppercase names and a production company credit, is placed vertically along the far right edge, printed in a muted grey. The overall finish suggests a high-quality lithographic print with areas of subtle texture, mimicking the raised grain of the brick wall and the slight gloss of the title letters. Every visible surface uses precise, grammatical English copy, declared in the final passage.;
This parallel theatrical poster for 'The Second Layer' employs a tactile, layered archaeology concept. The dominant image is an extreme, macro-scale close-up of the late-Qing painted scroll's surface, rendered with high-resolution photographic detail to show every frayed silk thread, cracked pigment flake, and stained silk warp. The scroll fills the entire poster as a monumental, textured plane. Upon this aged, amber-and-ochre ground, two adult Chinese hands are placed in intimate, active contact, becoming the protagonists. On the left, the mature, masculine hand of the scholar, his late forties evident in the slight prominence of veins and tendons. His hand rests palm-down, fingers splayed authoritatively over a section of painted, formal court architecture on the scroll. He wears a simple platinum band on his ring finger. His cuff is the pristine white cotton of a dress shirt, starched and precise. On the right, the younger, feminine hand of the conservator is actively engaged. Her hand is turned palm-up, her fingers gently but firmly tracing the contour of a painted, entwined figure beneath the surface. Her fingertips press into the silk, causing a slight depression in the material. Her wrist is bare, her skin smooth. The sleeve of her plum-colored silk blouse is pushed up to her forearm, its fabric rumpled and catching the light. The two hands do not touch each other directly but are connected through the shared medium of the scroll; his hand presses down from above, hers rises from below, their intentions meeting at the fragile surface. The composition is charged with tactile intimacy: the focus on skin, the tension between pressing and tracing, the contrast between his controlling flat palm and her receptive, curving fingers. Her thumb brushes the edge of a crack in the pigment, a gesture of discovery. The lighting is a single, raking spotlight from the upper left, casting long, dramatic shadows from each raised thread and creating hot highlights on the knuckles and silk. The palette is warm and earthy: the deep gold of aged silk, the burnt umber of shadows, the pale ochre of exposed ground, the flush of skin tones, and the vibrant accent of her plum sleeve. The typography is integrated as physical embossing and erosion. The title THE SECOND LAYER is debossed into the scroll's surface itself, as if the letters were once stamped with a seal and have left a permanent, recessed impression in the silk fibers. The letters are large, elegant serifs, filled with shadow, making them legible through light and texture rather than contrasting ink. They arc across the top third of the poster. The tagline, FEEL THE HISTORY THEY TRIED TO BURY, is rendered in faint, handwritten-style script in a dusty charcoal grey, as if annotated in the margin of the scroll, positioned near her wrist. The release line FOR YOUR CONSIDERATION appears in small, crisp type at the very bottom. The fictional billing block is presented as a tiny, typeset label reminiscent of a museum catalog entry, placed in the lower right corner on a faux-paper strip that appears slightly peeling. The poster's finish mimics a distressed lithograph, with visible paper grain and a subtle gloss varnish only on the embossed title areas. All visible text is in exact, grammatical English.;
The film poster is a vertical one-sheet centered on a tactile split-world concept, dividing the image with the frayed edge of a silk painting. In contemporary Shanghai, within a pristine, white-walled conservation studio in a high-rise building, the female art conservator stands on the modern side. She is a Chinese woman in her late twenties, with straight black hair tied in a low, professional bun. Her expression is one of focused restraint, her gaze directed downward at the work in front of her. She wears a tailored, cream-colored linen blouse, unbuttoned just enough to reveal the elegant line of her collarbone and the top of her sternum, and slim-fitting dark trousers. Her pose is one of delicate tension; her left hand hovers, fingers lightly spread, millimeters above the surface of a large, antique wooden conservation table, as if about to touch something precious. On the table lies the unfolded painted scroll, its surface a blend of formal, faded Qing-era portraiture and, visible through a large, deliberately torn section, the vibrant, intimate erotic scene beneath. Her right hand rests on the table's edge, knuckles white with subtle pressure. Across the physical tear in the image, the space transforms. The studio's clean lines dissolve into the warm, shadowed interior of a late-Qing chamber, suggested by a lacquered screen corner and the glow of a paper lantern. Here, the male mentor is present. He is a Chinese man in his late forties, with short, graying hair and thoughtful features. He wears a modern, unstructured charcoal-gray linen jacket over a simple black t-shirt. He is not looking at the woman but at the revealed painting, his body turned slightly away, creating a charged distance. His right hand is raised, index finger extended, pointing decisively at the erotic imagery, his gesture an act of scholarly revelation and insistence. The lighting is cinematic and directional, casting sharp shadows from the tear's edge. A cool, clinical light from large studio windows illuminates the conservator and the modern side, while a warm, amber glow emanates from the historical scene, catching the gloss of silk and the mentor's intense profile. The color palette is a duotone conflict: desaturated blues and whites for the present, rich cinnabar reds and golds for the past. The composition uses the torn silk as a diagonal force system, physically separating their worlds while connecting them through the shared artifact. The title 'THE SECOND LAYER' is typographic perspective, rendered as if painted in cracked, aged gold leaf directly onto the surface of the scroll, following the plane of the silk as it dips into the tear. The tagline 'A SECRET WAITS BENEATH THE SURFACE' appears in clean, modern sans-serif type, embossed into the white wall of the studio. The release line 'COMING SOON' and a compact fictional billing block are set in a crisp, small type at the very bottom, on a subtle letterpress-debossed strip. The image-text passage isolates all visible English copy: the main title, painted on the silk surface near the tear: THE SECOND LAYER; the tagline, embossed on the white studio wall to the right: A SECRET WAITS BENEATH THE SURFACE; the release line, at the bottom center: COMING SOON; the billing block, in a compact block at the bottom left: A FILM BY LIANG XIAO / STARRING ZHOU YAN & WEN JIAHAO / PRODUCED BY SILK ROAD PICTURES.;
This vertical poster employs a character-led prestige portrait with a visual substitution device. Set in the same contemporary Shanghai conservation studio at night, the scene is intimate and charged. The central image is a close-up, anti-portrait view of the female conservator's bare back. She is a Chinese woman in her late twenties, her pale skin glowing under a single, focused examination lamp. Her long black hair is loose, cascading over one shoulder and down her front. She kneels on a low, padded stool, her torso arched forward slightly, her spine a graceful, tense curve. Her arms are raised, her hands working with precise, delicate contact on the surface in front of her, which is not the scroll but the body of her mentor. The man lies on his stomach on the conservation table, a substitution where the table becomes a site of intimate revelation. He is a Chinese man in his late forties, his face turned to the side, eyes closed in a complex mix of concentration and surrender. His upper body is bare, revealing the sculpted planes of his back. Upon his skin, she is not applying pigment but carefully, slowly peeling away a layer of translucent, silk-like transfer film, mimicking the act of removing a painted surface. Where the film lifts, it reveals not his skin but a section of the erotic Qing scroll underneath-a tableau of entwined limbs and flowing fabric-visually tattooing the historical intimacy onto his present body. Her fingertips make direct, tactile contact with both the edge of the film and his skin, her touch intimate and professional. His body responds; the muscles of his back are relaxed yet engaged, his own hand resting near his head, fingers loosely curled. The lighting is dramatic and low-key, with the single lamp creating a high-contrast pool of light that isolates their connected forms, leaving the rest of the cluttered studio in deep shadow. The color palette is near-monochrome, dominated by warm blacks, sepia shadows, and the stark white of the transfer film, with the only narrative color being the cinnabar red and gold that bleeds from the revealed painting on his skin. The composition is radically bottom-weighted, the monumental forms of their bodies occupying the lower two-thirds of the poster, beneath a vast field of darkness. The title 'THE SECOND LAYER' acts as a typographic void. It is formed from the negative space of unlit studio wall at the top, the letters cut out by the precise shapes of hanging conservation tools-calipers, brushes, magnifying lenses-that cast sharp, linear shadows spelling the title in light gaps. The tagline 'WHAT WILL YOU UNCOVER?' is set in a fine, handwritten-style script that seems to be annotations in the margin of a lightbox, placed in the lower right. The release line 'COMING SOON' and a compact fictional billing block are integrated as tiny, embossed type on the edge of the table in the foreground. The image-text passage declares all visible English copy: the title, formed by negative space and tool shadows at the top: THE SECOND LAYER; the handwritten tagline, on the lightbox margin in the lower right: WHAT WILL YOU UNCOVER?; the release line, embossed on the table's front edge: COMING SOON; the billing block, on a small metal plaque on the table leg: ZHOU YAN * WEN JIAHAO * A LIANG XIAO FILM * VERTICAL ART STUDIOS.;
A high-resolution theatrical poster, designed as a single, monumental graphic object. The entire composition is the scroll itself, its silk frayed and edges torn, suspended against a deep matte black void. The painting within the scroll is not the historical erotic artwork, but a contemporary photograph of the two protagonists. The Chinese female art conservator, a woman in her late twenties with straight black hair tied in a low, neat bun, stands facing away, her posture reserved but her head turned slightly in profile. She wears a simple, high-collared linen shirt. The Chinese male scholar, a man in his late forties with short, graying hair and sharp glasses, stands parallel to her but facing forward, his gaze directed out at the viewer. He wears a tailored wool suit jacket. Their bodies are close but do not touch, separated by a hair's breadth of negative space that forms the silhouette of a paintbrush between them. The photograph is treated with a painterly glaze, its colors muted to sepia and burnt umber, mimicking aged pigments. Outside the painted rectangle, the raw silk of the scroll is rendered with hyper-realistic texture, showing loose threads and subtle water stains. The film's title, 'THE SECOND LAYER', is debossed into this silk margin in large, elegant serif capitals, the letters physically recessed so they catch the light from the upper left. The tagline 'WHAT DO YOU PRESERVE WHEN THE TRUTH IS NAKED?' is typeset in a clean, modern sans-serif, following the curve of the scroll's lower edge. At the very bottom, on a strip mimicking a museum label, are the words 'COMING SOON' and a compact, fictional billing block set in a tiny monospaced font. The lighting is a dramatic, raking spotlight from the top left, creating long shadows from the embossed title and highlighting the texture of the silk and the subtle contours of the painted figures within. The palette is almost monochromatic: blacks, browns, and creams, with the faintest hint of cinnabar red in the scholar's tie, the only narrative color. The poster is a study in restraint, tension, and layered revelation, where the central creative device is the scroll as both container and content, its physicality foregrounded to express the conflict between surface and substrate, professional duty and intimate truth.: At top center, debossed into the silk texture: THE SECOND LAYER; Curving along the bottom silk edge, in clean sans-serif: WHAT DO YOU PRESERVE WHEN THE TRUTH IS NAKED?; At bottom center, on a small museum-label strip: COMING SOON; Below that, in a tiny monospaced block: A FILM BY LI AN / ZHANG WEI / PRODUCED BY VERTEX PICTURES / JIA MING / FEI HONG / EDITOR ZHUO YU / MUSIC BY HSU / CINEMATOGRAPHY BY WEN LO;
A vertical one-sheet poster employing a radical negative-space revelation. The image is divided into two distinct zones by a sharp, horizontal line. The upper two-thirds depict a stark, contemporary conservation studio in Shanghai. A large, pristine lightbox table is the central feature, illuminated from within. On it lies a fragment of an old, bland official portrait, held flat by velvet weights. The Chinese female conservator, in her late twenties with her dark hair loosely tied back, wears a sleeveless silk tank top and linen trousers. She leans over the table, one hand placed flat on the glass surface near the portrait for stability, her body arched in focused attention. Her expression is one of intense, private discovery, her lips slightly parted. The lower third of the poster is dominated by deep shadow, but within this darkness, the negative space formed by the gap between her arm and torso, the curve of her waist, and the edge of the lightbox resolves into the clear, silhouetted shape of two intertwined bodies from the erotic scroll-a visual echo discovered only on second glance. This silhouette is not filled with detail but is a crisp, empty void punched through to a layer of warm, glowing amber light beneath, suggesting the hidden painting's passionate energy. The Chinese male scholar is present only indirectly: his hand, resting on the edge of the lightbox from the opposite side, enters the frame. He wears a crisp shirt with the sleeves rolled up, his fingers tapping thoughtfully near a spectrometer. This creates a tense, shared focus on the table between them. The title 'THE SECOND LAYER' is set in bold, translucent letters that appear to be laminated directly onto the glass of the lightbox, reflecting the cool studio lights. The tagline 'BENEATH THE SURFACE, DESIRE DEVELOPS' is printed in a fine, dry-transfer style along the ruler lying on the table. 'COMING SOON' and a compact fictional billing block are embossed subtly into the metallic frame of the lightbox at the bottom. Lighting is clinical and directional from above, creating sharp reflections on the glass and deep shadows below, masterfully guiding the eye from the factual upper world to the emotional revelation hidden in the negative space below.: Laminated onto the lightbox glass, reflective letters: THE SECOND LAYER; Printed along a ruler on the table: BENEATH THE SURFACE, DESIRE DEVELOPS; Embossed into the metal frame at bottom: COMING SOON; In a fine line below that: A LENS CAPITAL / WHITE PEONY FILMS PRODUCTION / DIRECTED BY SONG / STARRING JIN & MA / EXECUTIVE PRODUCERS FENG / COSTUME DESIGN LIU / SOUND DESIGN CHEN;
A dramatic vertical poster for the film 'The Second Layer' uses the concept of a translucent overlay. In a stark contemporary conservation studio in Shanghai, the Chinese woman conservator in her late twenties stands in profile, her expression a mix of scholarly focus and personal turmoil. She wears a simple, elegantly tailored black silk blouse, unbuttoned at the top to reveal her collarbone and the delicate chain of a necklace, and slim black trousers. Her dark hair is pulled back in a clean, severe bun. Her hands, raised before her, carefully hold up a large sheet of frosted plastic vellum, which carries the central image of the poster. On this translucent overlay, rendered in soft painterly glazes, is the scorching detail from the discovered Qing-era scroll: the entwined limbs of two lovers, their faces hidden, their bodies forming a sinuous, passionate knot against a background of faded silk. This erotic image is superimposed directly over the man, the renowned scholar in his late forties, who stands behind the vellum. He is dressed in a crisp light grey linen shirt and dark trousers, his posture authoritative yet conflicted. His gaze is fixed intently on the woman, not the image. The studio is minimalist; a steel conservation table holds tools under a single, precise spotlight that casts sharp shadows. The light passes through the vellum, projecting the ghostly painted lovers onto the wall behind them and partly across the man's chest, creating a literal and metaphorical second layer. The dominant color palette is cool greys and blacks, with the warm, glowing sepia and cinnabar reds of the painted overlay providing the only narrative color. The English title 'THE SECOND LAYER' is debossed into the concrete wall at left in large, severe sans-serif capitals, its letters filled with shadow, making it a physical void in the architecture. The tagline 'Every restoration reveals a secret' is set in crisp, small caps along the right edge of the vellum sheet itself, following its perspective. The release line 'COMING SOON' and a compact fictional billing block are aligned at the bottom in clean, minimalist type. The overall effect is one of luxurious restraint and tense revelation, where the story's erotic core is presented as fragile evidence caught between two people. This poster for 'The Second Layer' employs a radical vertical composition centered on the motif of torn-layer archaeology. The image is a tight, monumental close-up of the Chinese woman conservator's back, from her shoulders to her waist. She is seated, her posture taut with concentration. She wears only a delicate, cream-colored silk camisole, its thin straps fallen down her arms, leaving her shoulders and upper back bare. The fabric clings to her skin, revealing the elegant curve of her spine. Her dark hair is loosely gathered, with strands brushing her neck. The entire background is the surface of the painting she is working on: a large, distressed section of the bland official Qing portrait, its cracked beige and grey pigments dominating the frame. But across this surface, a dramatic tear has been made. A long, jagged strip of the top portrait layer has been carefully peeled back, revealing the vibrant, passionate erotic painting beneath. The revealed section shows a detailed fragment of the historical scene: a woman's bare back in an intimate embrace, a hand resting possessively on her hip, the brushstrokes fluid and alive. This revealed 'second layer' aligns perfectly with the living conservator's own bare back, creating a direct visual substitution where her body completes the historical image. The man, the scholar, is present only through his action and a partial reflection. His hand, masculine and steady, enters the frame from the left edge, holding a delicate conservation scalpel whose tip gently lifts the edge of the torn layer. In the reflective surface of a polished steel palette knife lying on the table, his focused, older face is dimly reflected, watching her work. The lighting is raking and directional, highlighting the incredible texture of the cracked varnish, the frayed silk fibers at the tear, and the sheen of sweat on the woman's skin. The palette is warm-umber, gold, and the flushed peach of skin-against the cool metallic reflection. The English title 'THE SECOND LAYER' is formed from the negative space of the tear itself; the missing paint creates the large, jagged letterforms that slice down the left side of the poster. The tagline 'What will you choose to uncover?' is hand-written in a precise, scholarly script along a faint pencil guideline visible on the intact portrait surface. The release line and billing block are printed in small, formal serif type at the very bottom, as if part of a museum label. This is an anti-portrait of intense intimacy and professional transgression, where the act of revelation is physically etched into the image. A vertical one-sheet for 'The Second Layer'. A Chinese woman in her late twenties, the art conservator, is depicted from the waist up, her back turned to the viewer. Her dark hair is in a neat, professional bun. She wears a contemporary, tailored white lab coat, unbuttoned and slipping off one shoulder to reveal the strap of a simple black camisole beneath and a smooth expanse of skin. Her posture is tense, one arm raised as if to reach forward, her fingers hovering just above the surface of the vast, unfurled Qing scroll that dominates the poster. The scroll, pinned to a large conservation easel, is a landscape of layered, translucent crimson and gold pigments depicting entwined, nude figures in an ancient pavilion; the painting glows with a warm, internal light that casts sharp shadows on the modern studio wall. Her older Chinese male mentor, in his late forties, stands just behind her right shoulder, out of focus in the shallow depth of field. He is dressed in a sharp grey wool blazer over a dark shirt. His gaze is fixed on the luminous scroll, his expression one of intense academic scrutiny. He does not touch her, but his proximity is charged, his presence a pressure in the composition. The light source comes from the painting itself, illuminating the conservator's profile, the elegant line of her neck and bare shoulder, and the fine texture of her white coat. The color palette is a stark contrast of cool studio greys against the warm, sensual golds and deep reds of the antique pigments. The film's English title, 'THE SECOND LAYER', is rendered in crisp, large serif capitals that appear to be physically embedded into the wooden frame of the easel, the letters cut deep as if carved, their shadows falling across the scroll's silk. The tagline 'A SECRET WAITS BENEATH THE SURFACE' is set in a lighter, smaller sans-serif type, aligned flush left along the bottom edge of the wooden frame. The release line 'COMING SOON' sits in the lower left corner in small, clean capitals. A compact, fictional billing block in a classic condensed typeface runs along the very bottom edge. The poster has a subtle textural finish, like matte paper with a slight glaze where the painted light hits, emphasizing the tactile contrast between modern fabric and ancient silk. Dominant central graphic idea: An iconic-object metaphor where the erotic historical scroll is a luminous, dangerous source of light and revelation, physically separating yet connecting the two contemporary figures through its glow and their shared, fraught gaze. The conservator's partially shed lab coat represents professional detachment being undone by personal, visceral discovery. A vertical one-sheet for 'The Second Layer' employing a typographic-monolith device. The entire poster background is a monumental, close-up texture of frayed, aged silk, its golden threads woven with subtle crimson streaks. Superimposed over this, the English title 'THE SECOND LAYER' is set in enormous, bold serif capitals that occupy ninety percent of the poster height. The letters are not solid; they are formed from negative space-precise cutouts in a deep, velvety black field that overlays the silk, allowing the luminous fabric to shine through as the letterforms themselves. Within the giant counter (the enclosed space) of the letter 'O', a small, sharp-focus narrative scene is revealed. Inside this circular window, we see the hands of the two Chinese protagonists. The woman's hands, with neatly trimmed nails, are delicately holding a fine conservation brush. The man's hands, older and with a scholar's steadiness, hold a magnifying lens. Their hands are not touching, but are positioned in intimate proximity over a tiny, exquisite detail of the Qing scroll visible beneath the lens: a painted curve of a nude figure's back. The woman's wrist is bare, her sleeve pushed up. The man's shirt cuff is precise and crisp. This intimate, technical collaboration is framed like a jewel within the massive 'O'. The rest of the black field and the other giant letters press in from all sides, creating a sense of discovery and claustrophobic focus. The color palette is minimal: black, the warm gold-and-crimson of the silk-text letters, and the neutral tones of skin and tools within the 'O'. Light falls sharply from the upper left, casting long shadows from the edges of the black cutout letters onto the silk below, adding dramatic depth. The tagline 'WHAT WILL YOU UNCOVER?' is set in small, crisp white sans-serif type, tucked into the lower right corner where the black field meets the silk. The release line 'COMING THIS FALL' is placed in the bottom left in matching small type. A minimal fictional billing block in a light grey condensed font runs along the very bottom. The poster's finish suggests a lush, tactile print with the black areas having a slight sheen and the silk texture appearing subtly raised. Dominant central graphic idea: Typography as a monumental void and framing device. The title's negative-space letters, cut from darkness, become windows that physically contain and constrain the story's core conflict-the intimate, charged act of joint examination. The 'O' acts as a lens, a keyhole, and a sealed world, emphasizing the theme of hidden layers and focused revelation. The scale contrast between the gigantic type and the tiny, precise human interaction creates intense visual tension. Visible copy locations and exact text: negative-space title letters cut from black field: THE SECOND LAYER; tagline lower right corner: WHAT WILL YOU UNCOVER?; release line bottom left: COMING THIS FALL; billing block along bottom page edge: ZHANG WEI - LI NA - ANNA CHEN - LUMINOUS PICTURES - SILK ROAD FILMS - PRODUCTION DESIGN BY HELEN ZHOU - MUSIC BY XIAO FENG - WWW.SECONDLAYER-MOVIE.COM;
A high-impact vertical theatrical poster for the fictional film 'The Second Layer'. In the sterile white light of a contemporary Shanghai art conservation lab, the Chinese female conservator, a woman in her late twenties with intelligent eyes and hair tied in a severe, professional knot, stands with her back to the viewer. She wears a form-fitting, high-collared sleeveless sheath dress of dove grey silk, the zipper at her back undone from the nape of her neck down to the small of her back, revealing a long, bare spine and shoulder blades. The Chinese male scholar, a man in his late forties with a distinguished, thoughtful face and closely trimmed hair, stands beside her, his gaze directed not at her body but at the luminous work table before them. His hand, clad in a white cotton conservation glove, rests lightly but decisively on the exposed skin of her lower back, his fingertips making precise, firm contact just above the waistline of her dress. His other hand is raised, holding a magnifying loupe over a large, unfurled painted scroll that dominates the table. The scroll is the central graphic device: its surface is a lush, abstract explosion of deep crimson, gold, and indigo painterly glazes, the pigments appearing wet and visceral, bleeding beyond the scroll's tattered silk edges to stain the lab's stainless steel table. This vibrant, erotic 'second layer' visually eats into the monochrome clinical space. The conservator's body is a tense line, her head tilted slightly as she looks down at the radiant pigments, her expression a mixture of profound apprehension and captivated awe. The composition is a split-world tension, divided diagonally by the stark edge of the lab table. Below the line lies the chaotic, colorful intimacy of the revealed scroll; above, the cold, ordered world of preservation. The man's touch bridges these two realms, an act of both professional guidance and intimate transgression. Lighting is harsh and fluorescent from above, casting sharp, clean shadows, but the scroll itself seems to emit a warm, internal glow that illuminates their faces and hands. The color palette is a stark duotone conflict: clinical whites, greys, and blues of the lab against the warm, saturated hemorrhage of the historical pigments. The English title 'THE SECOND LAYER' is set in a crisp, modern sans-serif, its letters physically embedded into the lab's wall as raised, backlit glass signage, with the vibrant scroll pigments visibly seeping into the negative spaces of the 'O' and 'E'. The tagline 'BENEATH EVERY MASTERPIECE, A SECOND TRUTH' is typeset in a smaller, elegant serif, aligned to the right edge, partially overlaid on the scroll's glow. The release line 'COMING SOON' and a compact, fictional English billing block are positioned in the lower left quadrant, printed in a muted grey that suggests letterpress debossing on the paper texture. The overall effect is one of severe, luxurious restraint, where a single intimate touch and a disruptive visual metaphor convey the film's core conflict between scholarly duty and vulnerable desire. On the scroll itself, the painted forms suggest abstracted, entangled bodies without literal depiction. A small, typed conservation label on the table edge reads: Item Log: Scroll 7B; a digital tablet screen nearby shows a spectral analysis graph. The poster's surface has a subtle texture of distressed lithography, with faint brushstrokes visible in the white fields, as if the poster itself is a conserved artifact.: wall-mounted glass sign: THE SECOND LAYER; lower right, over scroll glow: BENEATH EVERY MASTERPIECE, A SECOND TRUTH; lower left: COMING SOON; lower left, compact block: A FILM BY LIANG WEI * STARRING ANNA ZHANG AND MARK CHEN * EXECUTIVE PRODUCER JIAO YU * MUSIC BY ECHO STUDIOS * EDITOR WANG KAI; scroll label: Item Log: Scroll 7B; tablet screen: Spectral Analysis - Pigment Layer 2 - Active;
A vertical theatrical poster for 'The Second Layer' conceived as a tactile, forensic archive. The composition is an asymmetric editorial grid of three precise columns on a deep, warm off-white paper stock suggesting handmade cotton paper. The central and widest column is dominated by a monumental, tightly cropped image of the Chinese female conservator's bare back, from her mid-shoulders to just above the curve of her hips. She is turned away, her spine a pronounced, elegant line. The skin is rendered in high-resolution photographic detail, but overlaid upon it, like a translucent vellum sheet, is a precise technical diagram of the erotic Qing-era scroll, its silk support structure, pigment layers, and areas of loss mapped in fine red and black ink lines, creating a nested world where her body becomes the substrate for the historical artifact. Superimposed on this diagram, in one focused area just below her left shoulder blade, is a small, vivid photographic fragment of the scroll's actual painted imagery: a glimpse of a parted mouth in profile and a sensually curved neck. The Chinese male scholar is present only through evidence in the left column: a photographic close-up of his gloved hand holding an ultraviolet lamp, the eerie blue light falling across a sample swatch of frayed silk. The right column contains typography and archival fragments. This is a radical anti-portrait, telling the story through skin, trace, and technical data. The color palette is muted and archival: paper white, ink black, diagram red, and the faint blue of UV light, with the single shocking accent of the vivid flesh-and-crimson paint fragment on her skin. The English title 'THE SECOND LAYER' is the central typographic event, set in a severe, high-contrast modern serif. The letters are not printed but appear as deep, debossed impressions into the paper, their forms carved out by the negative space of the grid lines and the edge of the body photograph-a typographic void. Light rakes across the poster from the upper left, making the embossed title letters cast long, legible shadows that become the primary method of reading the title. The tagline 'WHAT WILL YOU UNCOVER?' is set in a clean sans-serif, positioned horizontally in the upper right column, aligned with the top of the hand photograph. The release line 'COMING SOON' and a succinct fictional English billing block are integrated into the bottom of the right column as typewritten lines on a strip of translucent tape that seems affixed to the paper. The poster's surface has the tactile quality of letterpress and layered collage, with visible paper fibers and the slight raised texture of the embossed title. It is a poster of controlled revelation, where intimacy is forensic and desire is mapped. A small red conservation seal is stamped in the lower left corner.: debossed paper impression, shadows form letters: THE SECOND LAYER; upper right column: WHAT WILL YOU UNCOVER?; right column bottom, on tape: COMING SOON; right column bottom, compact block: ANNA ZHANG * MARK CHEN * DIRECTED BY LIANG WEI * CINEMATOGRAPHY BY SONG MING * A STREAMSIDE PICTURES RELEASE; lower left corner, stamped: CONSERVATION ARCHIVE - LEVEL 2 ACCESS; diagram text: Silk Support * Vermillion Lake * Organic Binder; UV light sample label: Sample A7 - Fluorescence Response Positive;
A vertical one-sheet for the contemporary psychological thriller 'The Second Layer'. The poster's central graphic device is a monumental, distressed scroll of frayed raw silk hanging vertically in a stark, modern conservation studio in Shanghai. The scroll is semi-translucent, backlit by intense raking light that reveals the silhouettes of two Chinese adults, one female, one male, pressed against its inner surface from behind, their bodies oriented as if lying on the floor. The female conservator, in her late twenties, wears a simple, unbuttoned white lab coat over a black silk camisole; the male scholar, in his late forties, wears a partially opened dress shirt over dark trousers. Their profiles face each other but do not touch, the light casting their forms as ambiguous shadows trapped within the silk fibers, their hands pressed flat against the fabric from the inside, creating subtle indentations. The scroll's aged surface, dominating the composition, bears ghostly traces of painted peonies and the faint, red wax seal of the concubine artist. Across the face of the silk, the film's title appears not as ink but as if formed by thousands of micro-cracks in the old pigment. Cold, clinical LED work lights create sharp pools of white light on the steel lab tables, illuminating conservation tools but leaving the figures in a state of silhouetted suspension between exposure and concealment. The color palette is severely restricted: the warm, honeyed tone of the ancient silk against the cool grays and blues of the sterile studio, with the red of the seal and a single streak of crimson lake pigment bleeding from a crack as the only intense accents. Typography is integrated into the material reality of the scene: the title emerges from the damaged artifact itself; the tagline is set in a crisp, modern sans-serif that floats in the negative space of the dark floor like a reflection; the billing block is treated as a technical label printed on a translucent adhesive sheet partially affixed to the scroll's lower edge. The mood is one of tense, erotic archaeology, where the act of seeing is both a professional duty and a profound violation. This is a finished theatrical poster, not a still. English environmental copy is limited to fictional institutional labels on equipment and storage cabinets. A vertical one-sheet for 'The Second Layer' built around the conceptual device of a forensic diagram transformed into an intimate map. The poster presents a single, overhead view of a large, unfurled late-Qing painted scroll laid flat on a light table in a Shanghai archive at night. The scroll is the motif, its erotic central tableau-a concubine artist entwined with her lover-deliberately obscured under a sheet of semi-opaque drafting vellum. Overlaid on this vellum is a precise, technical diagram in fine red ink, mapping not the painting but the present-day bodies of the two Chinese conservators. The female conservator, in her late twenties, lies on her side atop the light table, partially undressed, her back arched, wearing only a silk skirt bunched at her waist. The male scholar, in his late forties, kneels beside her, one hand resting on her hip, the other pointing to a point on the diagram, his own shirt open. Their poses are still, studied, a living overlay to the hidden historical scene beneath them. The red diagram lines trace the contours of their current positions, labeling points of tension, pigment density, and silk stress with tiny, typed English notations, turning their intimacy into a conservator's analysis. The only light comes from the light table below, glowing through the vellum and the scroll, casting their skin in an ethereal blue-white and making the red ink diagram seem to float. The rest of the archival room recedes into deep shadow. The color palette is monochromatic: cool electronic blue from below, the stark white of the vellum and skin, and the single narrative color of the red ink. Typography is part of the diagrammatic system. The film's title is the largest annotation on the vellum, drawn in the same red ink as a header for the technical drawing. The tagline is incorporated as a handwritten note in the diagram's margin. The billing block is presented as a typed metadata strip along the bottom edge of the light table. The composition creates a powerful visual equation between historical discovery and personal exposure, treating desire as evidence to be cataloged. This is a finished theatrical poster, not a still. All readable text is in English. A cinematic vertical one-sheet poster, set in a contemporary Shanghai art conservation lab, uses negative-space revelation and a tactile, layered print aesthetic. The poster is dominated by a large, pristine examination table in sterile white light. At the center, a rectangular section of the table's surface is missing, torn away like aged paper to reveal a lush, painterly world beneath. From this 'tear,' the hidden late-Qing erotic scroll blooms upwards in vivid, saturated pigments of cinnabar red, lapis lazuli blue, and gold. The painted scene depicts two stylized, entwined nude figures in a flowing, lyrical composition, their bodies forming elegant arcs of desire against a silk-textured background. The Chinese female art conservator, Li Wen, a woman in her late twenties with intelligent eyes and her dark hair pulled into a practical bun, leans over the table. She is dressed in a simple, professional cream-colored linen shirt, the top buttons undone, revealing the curve of her neck and collarbone. Her hand, wearing a white cotton conservation glove, hovers just above the painted figures, her fingertips almost touching the glowing pigment in a gesture of suspended discovery and longing. Her expression is one of intense, captivated focus, her lips slightly parted. Standing behind her, slightly out of focus, is the Chinese male scholar, Professor Chen, a man in his late forties with sharp features and graying hair at his temples. He wears a tailored dark gray shirt, the sleeves rolled up. His hand rests firmly on the table's edge near the tear, his posture one of authoritative observation, but his gaze is fixed on Li Wen's profile, not the scroll, his expression a complex mix of academic ambition and personal concern. The lighting is dramatic: a cold, clinical spotlight illuminates the lab and the conservator, while a warm, diffuse glow emanates from the painted scroll beneath, casting a soft, sensual light upward onto Li Wen's face and throat. The color palette contrasts sterile blues and grays of the modern lab with the warm, radiant eruption of historical color and intimacy. The English title THE SECOND LAYER is rendered in a crisp, modern serif typeface, physically embedded into the lab's architecture. The letters are debossed into the white wall behind the table, catching the cold light on their top edges, with the warm glow from the scroll seeping into the recessed strokes. The tagline 'Beneath the surface lies a truth too intimate to preserve.' is set in a smaller, elegant italic serif, flowing along the bottom edge of the torn paper like a handwritten notation. The release line 'COMING SOON' is discreetly placed in the lower left, with a compact, fictional billing block in classic poster typography aligned to the right margin. A vertical theatrical poster employing a surreal transformation and typography-as-image concept, set against the stark, monumental interior of a Shanghai museum archival vault. The composition is an extreme close-up, focusing on the back of the Chinese female conservator, Li Wen. She is a woman in her late twenties, seen from behind, her posture tense. She wears a sleek, backless black silk dress-a departure from her professional attire-that reveals the full, elegant curve of her spine and shoulder blades. Her dark hair is down, cascading over one shoulder. The skin of her back becomes the canvas: across it, the intricate patterns and figures from the erotic scroll are projected, not as a flat image, but as if they are emerging from within her skin, a mixture of delicate painted lines and faint, glowing pigments. The Chinese male scholar, Professor Chen, stands facing her, a man in his late forties in a fine black wool suit, his shirt unbuttoned at the collar. He is not touching her, but his hand is raised, his fingers splayed as if he is the one casting the projection or tracing its outlines in the air mere inches from her skin. His expression is one of intense, scholarly scrutiny fused with undeniable desire, his gaze locked on the illuminated narrative unfolding across her back. Between them, suspended in the cool, dust-moted air of the vault, several strands of frayed historic silk thread from the scroll float, catching light. The environment is minimalist and imposing: towering steel shelves recede into darkness, emphasizing scale and isolation. The lighting is cinematic and directional, a single strong source from above-left creating deep shadows and highlighting the texture of silk and skin. The color palette is almost monochromatic-blacks, grays, and the warm taupe of skin-with the projected scroll elements providing the only color: faint washes of vermilion and gold. The English title THE SECOND LAYER is the central graphic device. The letters are formed from the very floating silk threads, arranged in mid-air as if by an invisible hand, some threads taut, others drooping, creating a fragile, three-dimensional typographic sculpture that exists between the two figures. The tagline 'Every restoration reveals a hidden self.' is set in a clean, small sans-serif, positioned vertically along the edge of a steel shelf. The release line 'COMING SOON' and a compact fictional billing block are integrated at the base of the poster, using a sharp, modern typeface that contrasts with the organic title. A vertical high-resolution theatrical poster for the fictional film 'The Second Layer'. The poster is a controlled environmental narrative tableau set inside a state-of-the-art conservation studio in modern Shanghai. The vast, sterile room, lined with halogen work lights and climate-controlled storage racks, recedes into a clinical one-point perspective. At the convergence point, the focal image is not the central character but the story's motif: a luminous, large-scale reproduction of the erotic Qing-dynasty scroll, its painted silk floating impossibly large against the sterile white wall. The scene is one of intimate forensic discovery and suppressed tension. The Chinese female conservator in her late twenties, Shen Wei, is positioned in the lower right foreground, seen in three-quarter profile. She is a mature adult with pale skin and dark hair tied in a severe, precise bun. She wears contemporary professional attire: a crisp, white lab coat over a simple, tailored black turtleneck. Her expression is one of profound, arrested focus, her gaze fixed on the scroll with a mixture of professional awe and personal recognition. Her posture is rigid, one hand clenched at her side, the other resting on the cool metal surface of a conservation table. The Chinese male scholar, Professor Lin, in his late forties, stands in the middle ground to the left, partially turned towards her. He is also a mature adult, with graying hair at his temples and sharp, intelligent features. He wears a well-cut dark gray wool suit and a white dress shirt, his professional authority clear. His expression is earnest and insistent, his mouth slightly open as if mid-argument. His hand gestures towards the monumental scroll, a physical vector connecting his professional ambition to the vulnerable intimacy on display. The scroll itself is rendered with exquisite painterly detail. The late-Qing concubine artist and her lover are depicted in delicate, fluid ink washes and faded mineral pigments, their nude bodies entwined in a composition of tender, reciprocal desire. Their forms are anatomically clear adult bodies, their poses one of mutual, active embrace and shared gaze, conveying conscious passion rather than objectification. The scroll's silk is shown with realistic fraying edges and subtle cracks in the pigment, a testament to time and concealment. The lighting is stark and directional, mimicking the precise beams of conservation lamps. It casts long, dramatic shadows from the equipment and the two figures across the polished concrete floor, creating a graphic system of lines that point toward the central image. The color palette is a severe duotone of cool grays, sterile whites, and the warm, aged sepia and cinnabar reds of the scroll, which acts as the single narrative color accent. The visual equation is clear: modern institutional order versus historic, liberated intimacy. The title 'THE SECOND LAYER' is integrated as a typographic monolith. It is set in a clean, monumental serif typeface, its letters rendered as if debossed into the studio's white wall itself, the shadows within the letterforms cast by the same directional light as the scene. The tagline 'BENEATH THE SURFACE LIES A DANGEROUS TRUTH' is set in a smaller, modern sans-serif, running vertically along the left edge like a column of archival text. The release line 'COMING THIS FALL' and a compact, fictional billing block are positioned at the very bottom in a crisp, small point size. The entire composition uses light, shadow, and scale to create a palpable tension between preservation and exposure, mirroring the film's core conflict.: The Second Layer; : Beneath the surface lies a dangerous truth; : Coming this fall; : A film by Liang Zhao * Starring Anya Chen and Michael Zhang * Executive Producer Sofia Wang * Cinematography by David Liu * Music by Elena Zhou * A Jade Dragon Pictures production;
A vertical high-resolution theatrical poster for 'The Second Layer' conceived as a tactile print object and intimate-object portrait. The poster simulates a one-of-a-kind letterpress print on thick, cotton-rag paper with visible deckle edges and a warm, off-white base. The central graphic device is an impossible scale metaphor: a single, life-sized human hand-belonging to Shen Wei, the Chinese female conservator-is shown in extreme, macro detail, its skin texture, fine lines, and the subtle dust of powdered pigment rendered with photographic realism. The hand holds a delicate, worn artist's brush, its tip stained with a vivid spot of cinnabar-red ink. This intimate detail becomes a landscape for the story. The erotic Qing scroll is not shown literally but is manifested through a negative-space revelation within the silhouette of her own hand and forearm. The negative space inside the curve of her thumb and the shadowed hollow of her palm forms the clear, recognizable contour of the two entwined lovers from the scroll, their embracing bodies defined only by the absence of ink, a ghost image in the paper's fiber. This creates a visual palindrome: the hand both restores and contains the secret. Shen Wei is present only through this fragment-a mature adult's hand, capable and careful. Professor Lin is represented anti-portrait style through his occupied space and a single prop: his own hand, masculine and older, enters the frame from the left edge, not touching hers but holding a pristine, white museum accession tag between his thumb and forefinger, its string dangling. The tag casts a sharp shadow over her wrist, a symbol of institutional claim. The interaction is one of erotic tension through withheld touch and parallel lines; their hands are close, the space between them charged with unspoken argument and magnetic pull. The backdrop is a field of layered, translucent paper textures suggesting centuries of archival silks and documents, with one subtle, embossed pattern of Qing textile motifs. The color palette is a warm tritone of paper white, deep sepia, and the single narrative accent of the cinnabar red on the brush tip. The title 'THE SECOND LAYER' is executed with typographic pressure, set in a classic letterpress typeface and printed with deep, physical impression into the paper, the ink slightly spread at the edges. It sits heavily at the top, feeling materially consequential. The tagline 'A SECRET WORTH UNRAVELING' is set in a finer, handwritten-style type that mimics personal annotation, placed along the right margin. The release line 'COMING SOON' and a compact, elegant fictional billing block are debossed modestly at the bottom. The poster's concept turns forensic conservation into an act of sensual, dangerous uncovering, where the tool in the conservator's hand and the empty space it defines become the story's most powerful actors.: The Second Layer; : A secret worth unraveling; : Coming soon; : Anya Chen * Michael Zhang * Directed by Liang Zhao * Produced by Sofia Wang * Editor: Marcus Koo * Production Designer: Fiona Ren * A Silk Road Studios film;
A vertical theatrical one-sheet poster rendered in a textural, painterly style where the boundaries between human skin, raw linen, and oil paint blur. The setting is a pristine, minimalist studio in a modern Beijing apartment, lit by the harsh, revealing glare of a single north-facing window. The composition is an asymmetric editorial grid, divided cleanly by a vertical line. On the left, the female painting instructor, a poised Chinese woman in her early thirties with dark hair tied in a severe but elegant knot, stands facing the viewer, her expression a complex mixture of defiance and vulnerability. Her posture is ceremonial, her arms held slightly away from her torso. Her bare skin is rendered with the tactile, brush-stroked quality of a painted figure, her form dissolving at the edges into the textured weave of a massive, unstretched canvas that occupies the right half of the poster. The canvas is pinned to the wall, its raw edges fraying into the studio space. Upon its surface, painted in a restrained palette of umber, ochre, and lead white, is a precise, life-size contour of the male collector's body-a Chinese man in his late forties-turned in profile. He is not physically present in the studio space; his representation exists only as this negative-space outline, a ghostly imprint defined by the absence of paint. His hand, however, breaches the division: his real, photographically detailed hand reaches from the right side of the poster, his fingers hovering just above the woman's shoulder, not quite touching, the proximity charged. The light casts a sharp shadow from his hand across her collarbone, a shadow that solidifies into the bold, serif letterforms of the film's title. The palette is austere: warm skin tones, cool grey concrete, the natural hues of linen and wood. The typography is integrated as physical intervention: the title emerges from the shadow narrative; the tagline is set in a crisp, modern sans-serif along the bottom edge of the canvas, aligned with its grid; the billing block is embossed subtly into the painted wall surface beside the window frame. A vertical theatrical poster conceived as a monumental, tactile print object, employing a stark duotone of deep indigo and metallic silver ink on thick, textured paper. From this macro view of artistic labor, a single, realistic human form emerges. The male collector, a Chinese man in his late forties with a precisely groomed appearance and an expression of intense, focused contemplation, lies on his back, his nude body conforming to the curved indentations of the palette's thumb hole and paint wells. His pose is one of voluntary offering, his arms resting at his sides, his gaze directed upward. His skin is rendered in the cool, reflective silver ink, making him appear both corporeal and manufactured, like a sculpted figure. Hovering above him, from the top of the frame, is the woman's hand-the Chinese female instructor in her early thirties. Her fingers, stained with indigo pigment, are reaching down. One fingertip makes deliberate, visible contact with the center of the man's chest, the indigo smear marking his skin. This touch is the central event, a point of connection that bridges the scales of creator and subject, tool and canvas. The composition is bottom-weighted, the massive palette filling the lower two-thirds, with vast negative space of raw paper above, broken only by the descending hand and the typography. The title is debossed into the paper at the top, its letters physically recessed, catching the light like a scar. The tagline is printed in a crisp, small indigo sans-serif that follows the curve of the palette's edge. The mood is one of intimate alchemy and reversed power dynamics, where the act of observation becomes a form of physical imprint. An anti-portrait poster for the erotic drama The Preservation Ritual. The concept is a negative-space revelation where the central image is formed by the absence of the two protagonists. The scene is set inside the sterile, climate-controlled vault of a museum in a humid southern Chinese city during summer. The cold, artificial light from a single overhead panel casts sharp, precise shadows. The visual focus is a large, pristine archival conservation mat, its crisp white rectangle dominating the vertical composition. Cut into the mat are two life-sized, anatomically clear negative silhouettes: one of a woman in her late twenties and one of a man in his forties, lying side-by-side as if resting on the floor. Their silhouettes are not touching but are parallel, their forms defined only by the rich, warm texture of the aged wooden floorboards visible through the cuts. Within the female silhouette, the grain of the wood subtly resembles the flowing lines of hair; within the male silhouette, it resembles the weave of fabric. The rest of the poster is a field of cool, institutional grey, emphasizing the vault's sealed atmosphere. The dominant creative device is the use of these precise voids as both absence and intense presence. The English title THE PRESERVATION RITUAL is set in a crisp, modern sans-serif, debossed into the grey field above the mat as if stamped by institutional pressure. The tagline 'What the archive remembers' is set in smaller, elegant serif type along the bottom edge of the white mat. The release line 'COMING SOON' and a compact fictional billing block are integrated into the grey border at the very bottom in a clean, minimal typeface. The palette is a severe contrast of sterile white, cool grey, and the warm, organic amber of the revealed wood. It is a poster of restraint, where the erotic tension is communicated through charged absence, intimate scale, and the palpable warmth bleeding through the cuts in a cold world. : The dominant title, debossed into the upper grey field: THE PRESERVATION RITUAL; : The tagline, set along the bottom edge of the white conservation mat: What the archive remembers; : The release line, in the bottom grey border: COMING SOON; : The fictional billing block, in the bottom grey border: A FILM BY LIANG WEI - STARRING ZHANG YAN & CHEN MING - PRODUCED BY SILENT SCROLL PICTURES;

A vertical theatrical one-sheet for the film 'The Clay and the Flame'. The entire poster is a close-up, tactile study of a single potter's wheel in motion, under the stark, focused beam of a single workshop spotlight that leaves the surrounding studio in deep shadow. The composition is a deliberate, radical crop, showing only from the mid-torso down to the wrists of the two potters. A Chinese woman in her late twenties, her expression one of intense, surrendered concentration, sits before the wheel. She is bare-shouldered, wearing only a sleeveless, clay-stained linen tank top that clings to her form; her forearms and hands are slick with dark, wet clay. Directly behind her, a Chinese man in his early fifties, the master, leans over her shoulder. He is shirtless, his mature, defined torso pressed against her back, his arms encircling her to guide her hands on the spinning clay. Their four hands-her smaller ones under his larger, guiding ones-are fused together in the gray slip, intimately molding the symmetrical curve of a tall, elegant vase form rising from the wheel head. The wet clay glistens under the light, its surface capturing the precise pressure of their combined touch. The dramatic chiaroscuro lighting carves their connected arms and the emerging vessel from the darkness, highlighting every ridge of muscle, every smear of clay, and the warm sheen of sweat on their skin. The color palette is monochrome earth and shadow: deep blacks, cool grays, and the warm, fleshy tone of skin, with the single narrative accent of the wet clay's dark umber. The title 'THE CLAY AND THE FLAME' is debossed in massive, sans-serif capitals across the top third of the poster, as if stamped into damp paper, its surface catching the same raking light as the clay. Beneath it, the tagline 'TO CREATE IS TO TOUCH' is set in clean, small caps, aligned left along the descending curve of the vase. The release line 'COMING SOON' and a compact, fictional billing block in a classic serif are positioned with severe precision in the lower left corner, integrated into the shadow. The design is a study of extreme intimacy through texture and guided force, an anti-portrait where the story is told entirely through hands, material, and shared physical intent.: THE CLAY AND THE FLAME: embossed-debossed sans-serif title, massive scale, horizontally centered across top third, catching raking light as if pressed into paper; TO CREATE IS TO TOUCH: clean small caps, aligned left, following the implied curve of the vase form, positioned just below the title's baseline; COMING SOON: classic serif, small, positioned in lower left corner within deep shadow; A FILM BY LI AN / ZHENG WEI: classic serif, tiny, stacked, directly below release line; WITH CHEN XIA / MASTER WU: classic serif, tiny, below director line; PRODUCED BY SILENT KILN PICTURES: classic serif, tiny, at very bottom left edge;

The two figures are not physically touching; the intimacy is conveyed through the shared, containing shape of the cocoon and the analytical yet intimate language of the diagram that connects them.
